import * as acorn from 'acorn';
import * as babel from '@babel/parser';

const cases = [
  ['using foo = null', {sourceType:'module', ecmaVersion:'latest'}],
  ['await using foo = null', {sourceType:'module', ecmaVersion:'latest'}],
  ['function f(){ using x = 1, y = 2; }', {sourceType:'script', ecmaVersion:'latest'}],
  ['async function f(){ await using x = 1; }', {sourceType:'script', ecmaVersion:'latest'}],
  ['for (using x of y) {}', {sourceType:'module', ecmaVersion:'latest'}],
  ['for (await using x of y) {}', {sourceType:'module', ecmaVersion:'latest'}],
  ['async function f(){ for await (using x of y) {} }', {sourceType:'script', ecmaVersion:'latest'}],
  ['async function f(){ for await (await using x of y) {} }', {sourceType:'script', ecmaVersion:'latest'}],
];

for (const [code, opts] of cases) {
  console.log('===', JSON.stringify(code));
  try {
    const ast = acorn.parse(code, {ecmaVersion:'latest', sourceType: opts.sourceType});
    console.log('acorn:', JSON.stringify(ast.body, (k,v)=> k==='start'||k==='end'?undefined:v, 2).slice(0, 1200));
  } catch(e) { console.log('acorn err', e.message); }

    const ast = babel.parse(code, {sourceType: opts.sourceType, plugins: ['explicitResourceManagement']});
    console.log('babel:', JSON.stringify(ast.program.body, (k,v)=> k==='start'||k==='end'||k==='loc'?undefined:v, 2).slice(0, 1200));
  } catch(e) { console.log('babel err', e.message); }
}

     8	export const enum ScopeKind {
     9	  ForStatement = 1 << 0,
    10	  Block = 1 << 1,
    11	  CatchStatement = 1 << 2,
    12	  SwitchStatement = 1 << 3,
    13	  TryStatement = 1 << 4,
    14	  CatchBlock = 1 << 5,

    23	  type: Errors;
    24	  params: string[];

    28	
    29	export class Scope {
    30	  // Some scopeError doesn't necessarily fail parsing.
    31	  // For example function a(dup, dup) {} is fine,
    32	  // But duplicated params is not allowed in strict mode,
    33	  // So function a(dup, dup) { "use strict" } would fail.
    34	  // Retain the scopeError on scope for later decision.
    35	  scopeError?: ScopeError;
    36	
    37	  variableBindings = new Map<string, BindingKind>();

    39	  constructor(

    42	    public readonly parent?: Scope,

    44	
    45	  createChildScope(type?: ScopeKind) {
    46	    return new Scope(this.parser, type, this);
    47	  }
    48	
    49	  /**
    50	   * Adds either a var binding or a block scoped binding.
    51	   *
    52	   * @param context Context masks
    53	   * @param name Binding name
    54	   * @param type Binding kind
    55	   * @param origin Binding Origin
    56	   */
    57	  addVarOrBlock(context: Context, name: string, kind: BindingKind, origin: Origin) {
    58	    if (kind & BindingKind.Variable) {
    59	      this.addVarName(context, name, kind);
    60	    } else {
    61	      this.addBlockName(context, name, kind, origin);
    62	    }
    63	    if (origin & Origin.Export) {
    64	      this.parser.declareUnboundVariable(name);
    65	    }
    66	  }
    67	
    68	  /**
    69	   * Adds a variable binding
    70	   *
    71	   * @param context Context masks
    72	   * @param name Binding name
    73	   * @param type Binding kind
    74	   */
    75	  addVarName(context: Context, name: string, kind: BindingKind): void {
    76	    const { parser } = this;
    77	    // eslint-disable-next-line @typescript-eslint/no-this-alias
    78	    let currentScope: Scope | undefined = this;
    79	
    80	    while (currentScope && (currentScope.type & ScopeKind.FunctionRoot) === 0) {
    81	      const { variableBindings } = currentScope;
    82	      const value = variableBindings.get(name);
    83	
    84	      if (value && value & BindingKind.LexicalBinding) {
    85	        if (
    86	          parser.options.webcompat &&
    87	          (context & Context.Strict) === 0 &&
    88	          ((kind & BindingKind.FunctionStatement && value & BindingKind.LexicalOrFunction) ||
    89	            (value & BindingKind.FunctionStatement && kind & BindingKind.LexicalOrFunction))
    90	        ) {
    91	          // No op
    92	        } else {
    93	          parser.report(Errors.DuplicateBinding, name);
    94	        }
    95	      }
    96	      if (currentScope === this) {
    97	        if (value && value & BindingKind.ArgumentList && kind & BindingKind.ArgumentList) {
    98	          currentScope.recordScopeError(Errors.DuplicateBinding, name);
    99	        }
   100	      }
   101	      if (
   102	        value &&
   103	        (value & BindingKind.CatchPattern || (value & BindingKind.CatchIdentifier && !parser.options.webcompat))
   104	      ) {
   105	        parser.report(Errors.DuplicateBinding, name);
   106	      }
   107	
   108	      currentScope.variableBindings.set(name, kind);
   109	
   110	      currentScope = currentScope.parent;
   111	    }
   112	  }
   113	
   114	  hasVariable(name: string) {
   115	    return this.variableBindings.has(name);
   116	  }
   117	
   118	  /**
   119	   * Adds block scoped binding
   120	   *
   121	   * @param context Context masks
   122	   * @param name Binding name
   123	   * @param type Binding kind
   124	   * @param origin Binding Origin
   125	   */
   126	  addBlockName(context: Context, name: string, kind: BindingKind, origin: Origin) {
   127	    const { parser } = this;
   128	    const value = this.variableBindings.get(name);
   129	
   130	    if (value && (value & BindingKind.Empty) === 0) {
   131	      if (kind & BindingKind.ArgumentList) {
   132	        this.recordScopeError(Errors.DuplicateBinding, name);
   133	      } else if (
   134	        parser.options.webcompat &&
   135	        (context & Context.Strict) === 0 &&
   136	        origin & Origin.BlockStatement &&
   137	        value === BindingKind.FunctionLexical &&
   138	        kind === BindingKind.FunctionLexical
   139	      ) {
   140	        // No op
   141	      } else {
   142	        parser.report(Errors.DuplicateBinding, name);
   143	      }
   144	    }
   145	
   146	    if (
   147	      this.type & ScopeKind.FunctionBody &&
   148	      this.parent?.hasVariable(name) &&
   149	      (this.parent.variableBindings.get(name)! & BindingKind.Empty) === 0
   150	    ) {
   151	      parser.report(Errors.DuplicateBinding, name);
   152	    }
   153	
   154	    if (this.type & ScopeKind.ArrowParams && value && (value & BindingKind.Empty) === 0) {
   155	      if (kind & BindingKind.ArgumentList) {
   156	        this.recordScopeError(Errors.DuplicateBinding, name);
   157	      }
   158	    }
   159	
   160	    if (this.type & ScopeKind.CatchBlock) {
   161	      if (this.parent!.variableBindings.get(name)! & BindingKind.CatchIdentifierOrPattern)
   162	        parser.report(Errors.ShadowedCatchClause, name);
   163	    }
   164	
   165	    this.variableBindings.set(name, kind);
   166	  }
   167	
   168	  /**
   169	   * Record duplicate binding errors that may occur in a arrow head or function parameters
   170	   *
   171	   * @param parser Parser state
   172	   * @param type Errors type
   173	   */
   174	  recordScopeError(type: Errors, ...params: string[]) {
   175	    this.scopeError = {
   176	      type,
   177	      params,
   178	      start: this.parser.tokenStart,
   179	      end: this.parser.currentLocation,
   180	    };

STEP 53 SOURCE agent
REASONING_CONTENT
For using declarations, we want the binding to be an Identifier only. The detection at statement start should only trigger if next token is an
identifier (not pattern). But for `using {x} = foo`, Babel detects it as a using declaration (because `{` is included in detection), then errors
"cannot have destructuring". So detection should include `{` (LeftBrace) but not `[` (LeftBracket). Then in parseVariableDeclaration, if the parsed id
is a pattern, report "cannot have destructuring".

Actually, let me reconsider. The task says:
- "Destructuring pattern: 'cannot have destructuring'"

This means our error message must contain "cannot have destructuring". It doesn't specify which forms trigger it. To be safe and follow Babel, I'll:
- Detect `using` as declaration when followed on same line by a binding identifier OR `{` (LeftBrace), but NOT `[`.
- When parsing using declaration, only allow identifier bindings; if the next token is `{`, parse it as a pattern and then report destructuring error.
Or simpler: if next token is `{` at detection time, still enter declaration parsing, and in `parseVariableDeclaration`/`parseVariableDeclarationList`
report destructuring error before parsing the pattern.

Let me check Acorn's behavior for `using {x}` — acorn reports "Unexpected token" because acorn's `isUsing` only checks identifier start (not `{`), so
`using {x}` is treated as expression and fails. Babel is better here (gives destructuring error). We'll follow Babel since the task explicitly
mentions destructuring error.

Now, what about `for (using {x} of y)`? Let me test Babel.

using declaration binding `of`. In for-head, `for (using of = x; ;)` is a using declaration (binding `of`), but `for (using of y)` is a for-of loop
where `using` is the iterated identifier. So the special check: if next word is `of` and the character after `of` is not `=`, `:`, `;`, then treat as

### Detailed implementation plan

1. **token.ts**: Add `UsingKeyword` token. It should be a contextual keyword with `IsExpressionStart | IsIdentifier` so it can start
expressions/statements and be used as an identifier. Let's add:
```ts
UsingKeyword = 138 | Contextual | IsExpressionStart | IsIdentifier,
```
But wait — does `Contextual` imply `IsIdentifier` already? Looking at existing definitions: `Contextual = 1 << 13 | Keyword`, `IsIdentifier = 1 << 17
| Contextual`. So `IsIdentifier` includes `Contextual`. Existing contextual keywords: `AsKeyword = 108 | Contextual | IsExpressionStart`,
`AsyncKeyword = 109 | Contextual | IsExpressionStart | IsIdentifier`, `AwaitKeyword = 110 | Contextual | IsExpressionStart | IsIdentifier`. So
`IsIdentifier` is redundant with `Contextual` but they include it. I'll do `UsingKeyword = 138 | Contextual | IsExpressionStart | IsIdentifier`.

Also add to `KeywordDescTable` (the string description array) and `descKeywordTable` mapping `'using': Token.UsingKeyword`.

Wait, but there's a subtlety: the `descKeywordTable` lookup happens for all identifiers in `scanIdentifier`. If we add `using` as a contextual
keyword, then every occurrence of `using` in code (including as a normal identifier) becomes `Token.UsingKeyword`. That's fine — it behaves like
`await`, `async`, `of`, etc. (contextual keywords that are still identifiers in expression position). The parser's `parseIdentifier` will accept it
because it has `IsIdentifier`.

2. **common.ts**: Add `BindingKind.Using` and `BindingKind.AwaitUsing`. Include them in `LexicalBinding` so duplicate detection works.

3. **errors.ts**: Add new error enum entries and messages:
```ts
UsingDeclarationGlobalScope, // "'using' declaration is not allowed in the global scope"
AwaitUsingOutsideAsync, // "'await using' is only allowed inside async functions and modules"
UsingMissingInitializer, // "'using' declarations must have an initializer"
ForInUsing, // "'using' declarations are not allowed in for-in loops"
UsingHasBindingPattern, // "Using declaration cannot have destructuring patterns"
```

Let me pick exact messages that contain required substrings:
- Script global scope: "Using declaration is not allowed in the global scope" — contains "not allowed in the global scope".
- Await using outside async/module: "'await using' is only allowed inside async functions and modules" — contains "only allowed inside async".
- Missing initializer: "Using declaration must have an initializer" — contains "must have an initializer".
- For-in: "Using declaration is not allowed in for-in loops" — contains "not allowed in for-in".
- Destructuring: "Using declaration cannot have destructuring patterns" — contains "cannot have destructuring".

4. **estree.ts**: Update `VariableDeclaration.kind` to include `'using' | 'await using'`.

5. **parser.ts**:
   a. In `parseStatementListItem`, add cases for `Token.UsingKeyword` and `Token.AwaitKeyword`.
   - For `Token.UsingKeyword`: call a new `parseUsingIdentOrVarDeclarationStatement(parser, context, scope, privateScope, origin)`.
   - For `Token.AwaitKeyword`: need to detect `await using`. Since detection requires lookahead beyond `await`, we can write a function that consumes
`await` as identifier (parseIdentifier), then checks if next token is `UsingKeyword` on same line, then checks if token after using is identifier. But
that's two-token lookahead. We can handle it by: if current is `AwaitKeyword`, consume it as identifier, then if next is `UsingKeyword` and no
newline, parse `using` as identifier too (parseIdentifier), then if the following token is a binding identifier (and no newline between using and it),
it's `await using` declaration. Otherwise fall back to building an expression from `await` identifier + using identifier? But wait, we've already
consumed `await` and `using`. If it's not a declaration, we need to treat them as expressions: `await using` without a binding — e.g., `await using;`
— should be `await` identifier, then `using` identifier as separate expression statements? Or `await using` could be an await expression of `using` in
async context! E.g., `async function f() { await using; }` — here `await` is an await expression applied to identifier `using`. So we need to be
careful.

Let me think about how to detect `await using` declaration without losing the ability to parse `await` as expression.

Option: Write a lookahead helper that peeks at raw source (like Babel/acorn do) to determine if `await` is followed by `using` followed by binding
identifier on the same lines. This avoids consuming tokens. The parser has access to source and positions. Let me examine the Parser class to see
available fields.

import { type AssignmentKind, type DestructuringKind, Flags, type Location } from '../common';
import { Errors, ParseError } from '../errors';
import type * as ESTree from '../estree';
import { convertTokenType } from '../lexer';
import { type NormalizedOptions, normalizeOptions, type OnComment, type OnToken, type Options } from '../options';
import { Token } from '../token';
import { PrivateScope } from './private-scope';
import { Scope, type ScopeKind } from './scope';

export class Parser {
  private lastOnToken: [string, number, number, ESTree.SourceLocation] | null = null;

  options: NormalizedOptions;

  token = Token.EOF;
  /**
   * The mutable parser flags, in case any flags need passed by reference.
   */
  flags = Flags.None;
  /**
   * The current index
   */
  index = 0;
  /**
   * Beginning of current line
   */
  line = 1;

  /**
   * Beginning of current column
   */
  column = 0;

  /**
   * Start position of whitespace/comment before current token
   */
  startIndex = 0;

  /**
   * The end of the source code
   */
  end = 0;

  /**
   * Start position of text of current token
   */
  tokenIndex = 0;

  /**
   * Start position of the column before newline
   */
  startColumn = 0;

  /**
   * Position in the input code of the first character after the last newline
   */
  tokenColumn = 0;

  /**
   * The number of newlines
   */
  tokenLine = 1;

  /**
   * Start position of text of current token
   */
  startLine = 1;

  /**
   * Holds the scanned token value
   */
  tokenValue: any = '';

  /**
   * Holds the raw text that have been scanned by the lexer
   */
  tokenRaw = '';

  /**
   * Holds the regExp info text that have been collected by the lexer
   */
  tokenRegExp: void | {
    pattern: string;
    flags: string;
  } = void 0;

  /**
   * The code point at the current index
   */
  currentChar = 0;

  /**
   *  https://tc39.es/ecma262/#sec-module-semantics-static-semantics-exportednames
   */
  exportedNames = new Set<string>();

  /**
   * https://tc39.es/ecma262/#sec-exports-static-semantics-exportedbindings
   */

  exportedBindings = new Set<string>();

  /**
   * Assignable state
   */
  assignable: AssignmentKind | DestructuringKind = 1;

  /**
   * Destructuring state
   */
  destructible: AssignmentKind | DestructuringKind = 0;

  /**
   * Holds leading decorators before "export" or "class" keywords
   */
  leadingDecorators: {
    start?: Location;
    decorators: ESTree.Decorator[];
  } = { decorators: [] };

  constructor(
    /**
     * The source code to be parsed
     */
    public readonly source: string,
    rawOptions: Options = {},
  ) {
    this.end = source.length;
    this.currentChar = source.charCodeAt(0);
    this.options = normalizeOptions(rawOptions);

    // Accepts either a callback function to be invoked or an array to collect comments (as the node is constructed)
    if (Array.isArray(this.options.onComment)) {
      this.options.onComment = pushComment(this.options.onComment, this.options);
    }

    // Accepts either a callback function to be invoked or an array to collect tokens
    if (Array.isArray(this.options.onToken)) {
      this.options.onToken = pushToken(this.options.onToken, this.options);
    }
  }

  /**
   * Get the current token in the stream to consume
   * This function exists as workaround for TS issue
   * https://github.com/microsoft/TypeScript/issues/9998
   */
  getToken() {
    return this.token;
  }

  /**
   * Set the current token in the stream to consume
   * This function exists as workaround for TS issue
   * https://github.com/microsoft/TypeScript/issues/9998
   */
  setToken(value: Token, replaceLast = false) {
    this.token = value;

    const { onToken } = this.options;

    if (onToken) {
      if (value !== Token.EOF) {
        const loc = {
          start: {
            line: this.tokenLine,
            column: this.tokenColumn,
          },
          end: {
            line: this.line,
            column: this.column,
          },
        };

        if (!replaceLast && this.lastOnToken) {
          onToken(...this.lastOnToken);
        }
        this.lastOnToken = [convertTokenType(value), this.tokenIndex, this.index, loc];
      } else {
        if (this.lastOnToken) {
          onToken(...this.lastOnToken);
          this.lastOnToken = null;
        }
      }
    }
    return value;
  }

  get tokenStart(): Location {
    return {
      index: this.tokenIndex,
      line: this.tokenLine,
      column: this.tokenColumn,
    };
  }

  get currentLocation(): Location {
    return { index: this.index, line: this.line, column: this.column };
  }

  finishNode<T extends ESTree.Node>(node: T, start: Location, end: Location | void): T {
    if (this.options.ranges) {
      node.start = start.index;
      const endIndex = end ? end.index : this.startIndex;
      node.end = endIndex;
      node.range = [start.index, endIndex];
    }

    if (this.options.loc) {
      node.loc = {
        start: {
          line: start.line,
          column: start.column,
        },
        end: end ? { line: end.line, column: end.column } : { line: this.startLine, column: this.startColumn },
      };

      if (this.options.source) {
        node.loc.source = this.options.source;
      }
    }

    return node;
  }

  /**
   * Appends a name to the `ExportedBindings` of the `ExportsList`,
   *
   * @see [Link](https://tc39.es/ecma262/$sec-exports-static-semantics-exportedbindings)
   *
   * @param name Exported binding name
   */
  addBindingToExports(name: string): void {
    this.exportedBindings.add(name);
  }

  /**
   * Appends a name to the `ExportedNames` of the `ExportsList`, and checks
   * for duplicates
   *
   * @see [Link](https://tc39.github.io/ecma262/$sec-exports-static-semantics-exportednames)
   *
   * @param name Exported name
   */
  declareUnboundVariable(name: string): void {
    const { exportedNames } = this;

    if (exportedNames.has(name)) {
      this.report(Errors.DuplicateExportBinding, name);
    }

    exportedNames.add(name);
  }

  /**
   * Throws an error
   *
   * @export
   * @param {Errors} type
   * @param {...string[]} params
   * @returns {never}
   */
  report(type: Errors, ...params: string[]): never {
    throw new ParseError(this.tokenStart, this.currentLocation, type, ...params);
  }

  createScopeIfLexical(type?: ScopeKind, parent?: Scope) {
    if (this.options.lexical) {
      return this.createScope(type, parent);
    }

    return undefined;
  }

  createScope(type?: ScopeKind, parent?: Scope) {
    return new Scope(this, type, parent);
  }

  createPrivateScopeIfLexical(parent?: PrivateScope) {
    if (this.options.lexical) {
      return new PrivateScope(this, parent);
    }

    return undefined;
  }

  cloneIdentifier(original: ESTree.Identifier): ESTree.Identifier {
    return this.cloneLocationInformation({ ...original }, original);
  }

  cloneStringLiteral(original: ESTree.StringLiteral): ESTree.StringLiteral {
    return this.cloneLocationInformation({ ...original }, original);
  }

  private cloneLocationInformation<T extends ESTree.Node>(node: T, original: T) {
    if (this.options.ranges) {
      node.range = [...original.range!];
    }

    if (this.options.loc) {
      node.loc = {
        ...original.loc,
        start: { ...original.loc!.start },
        end: { ...original.loc!.end },
      };
    }

    return node;
  }
}

function pushComment(comments: ESTree.Comment[], options: NormalizedOptions): OnComment {
  return function (type: ESTree.CommentType, value: string, start: number, end: number, loc: ESTree.SourceLocation) {
    const comment: ESTree.Comment = {
      type,
      value,
    };

    if (options.ranges) {
      comment.start = start;
      comment.end = end;
      comment.range = [start, end];
    }
    if (options.loc) {
      comment.loc = loc;
    }
    comments.push(comment);
  };
}

function pushToken(tokens: Token[], options: NormalizedOptions): OnToken {
  return function (type: string, start: number, end: number, loc: ESTree.SourceLocation) {
    const token: any = {
      token: type,
    };

    if (options.ranges) {
      token.start = start;
      token.end = end;
      token.range = [start, end];
    }

    if (options.loc) {
      token.loc = loc;
    }
    tokens.push(token);
  };
}
---UTIL---
export function getOwnProperty<T>(object: Record<string | number, T>, key: string | number): T | undefined {
  return Object.hasOwn(object, key) ? object[key] : undefined;
}

```ts
function isUsingDeclaration(parser: Parser, isAwaitUsing: 0 | 1, isFor: 0 | 1): boolean {
  if (parser.options.next !== true) return false; // only when next: true
  if (isAwaitUsing) {
    if (parser.getToken() !== Token.AwaitKeyword) return false;
  } else {
    if (parser.getToken() !== Token.UsingKeyword) return false;

}
```

import { Chars } from '../chars';
import { Flags } from '../common';
import { type Parser } from '../parser/parser';
import { Token } from '../token';

export const enum LexerState {
  None = 0,
  NewLine = 1 << 0,
  LastIsCR = 1 << 2,
}

export const enum NumberKind {
  ImplicitOctal = 1 << 0,
  Binary = 1 << 1,
  Octal = 1 << 2,
  Hex = 1 << 3,
  Decimal = 1 << 4,
  NonOctalDecimal = 1 << 5,
  Float = 1 << 6,
  ValidBigIntKind = 1 << 7,
  DecimalNumberKind = Decimal | NonOctalDecimal,
}

/**
 * Advances this lexer's current index.
 *
 * @param parser The parser instance
 */
export function advanceChar(parser: Parser): number {
  parser.column++;
  return (parser.currentChar = parser.source.charCodeAt(++parser.index));
}

/**
 * Consumes possible surrogate pair, return the merged code point or 0
 *
 * @param parser The parser instance
 */
export function consumePossibleSurrogatePair(parser: Parser): number {
  // See: https://tc39.github.io/ecma262/#sec-ecmascript-language-types-string-type
  const hi = parser.currentChar;
  // Not surrogate
  if ((hi & 0xfc00) !== Chars.LeadSurrogateMin) return Chars.Null;
  const lo = parser.source.charCodeAt(parser.index + 1);
  // Unpaired surrogate
  if ((lo & 0xfc00) !== Chars.TrailSurrogateMin) return Chars.Null;
  // Merged code point
  return Chars.NonBMPMin + ((hi & 0x3ff) << 10) + (lo & 0x3ff);
}

/**
 * Use to consume a line feed instead of `scanNewLine`.
 */
export function consumeLineFeed(parser: Parser, state: LexerState): void {
  parser.currentChar = parser.source.charCodeAt(++parser.index);
  parser.flags |= Flags.NewLine;
  if ((state & LexerState.LastIsCR) === 0) {
    parser.column = 0;
    parser.line++;
  }
}

export function scanNewLine(parser: Parser): void {
  parser.flags |= Flags.NewLine;
  parser.currentChar = parser.source.charCodeAt(++parser.index);
  parser.column = 0;
  parser.line++;
}

// ECMA-262 11.2 White Space
// gC=Zs, U+0009, U+000B, U+000C, U+FEFF
export function isExoticECMAScriptWhitespace(ch: number): boolean {
  return (
    ch === Chars.NonBreakingSpace ||
    ch === Chars.ZeroWidthNoBreakSpace ||
    ch === Chars.NextLine ||
    ch === Chars.Ogham ||
    (ch >= Chars.EnQuad && ch <= Chars.ZeroWidthSpace) ||
    ch === Chars.NarrowNoBreakSpace ||
    ch === Chars.MathematicalSpace ||
    ch === Chars.IdeographicSpace ||
    ch === Chars.ThinSpace ||
    ch === Chars.ByteOrderMark
  );
}

/**
 * Converts a value to a hex value
 *
 * @param code CodePoint
 */
export function toHex(code: number): number {
  return code < Chars.UpperA ? code - Chars.Zero : (code - Chars.UpperA + 10) & 0xf;
}

/**
 * Converts a token to a string representation
 *
 * @param t Token
 */
export function convertTokenType(t: Token): string {
  switch (t) {
    case Token.NumericLiteral:
      return 'NumericLiteral';
    case Token.StringLiteral:
      return 'StringLiteral';
    case Token.FalseKeyword:
    case Token.TrueKeyword:
      return 'BooleanLiteral';
    case Token.NullKeyword:
      return 'NullLiteral';
    case Token.RegularExpression:
      return 'RegularExpression';
    case Token.TemplateContinuation:
    case Token.TemplateSpan:
    case Token.Template:
      return 'TemplateLiteral';
    default:
      if ((t & Token.IsIdentifier) === Token.IsIdentifier) return 'Identifier';
      if ((t & Token.Keyword) === Token.Keyword) return 'Keyword';

      return 'Punctuator';
  }
}

There's no existing source-lookahead utility. But we can implement a simple one in parser.ts. The parser knows `parser.index` (current scan position,
i.e., after the current token) and `parser.source`. We can scan forward from `parser.index`, skipping whitespace/comments, and check for line breaks.

But wait — at statement start, the current token is `using` or `await`, and `parser.index` points just after the current token. So we can scan forward
from `parser.index` to find the next word.

Let me write a helper `scanAhead` that:
- Starts at `parser.index`.
- Skips whitespace and comments (using existing comment skipping functions? They mutate parser state though; we need non-mutating). Let me write a
simple scanner.

Actually, simpler: I can write a helper that only needs to check the text immediately after the current token, ignoring whitespace/comments. But
comments can contain line breaks, so we need to account for them. For a robust implementation, let me write a small non-mutating scanner that:
- Given `parser`, start at `pos = parser.index`.
- Loops: skip whitespace; if `//` or `/*` comment, skip it (tracking line breaks); continue. If newline encountered, return with `hasLineBreak =
true`.
- Then reads the next identifier word and returns `{ word, hasLineBreakBefore }`.

STEP 63 SOURCE agent
REASONING_CONTENT
The existing parser uses one-token lookahead via consuming. For `await using`, we need two-token lookahead. Let me see how `parseIdentifierOrArrow`
works — it consumes `await` as identifier and checks the next token. If we consume `await` and then see `using`, we can consume `using` and check the
token after. But if it's not a declaration, we'd need to re-parse from `await`.

Actually, let's think differently. We can implement detection via source lookahead, which is what Babel/acorn do and is cleaner. Let me write a helper
in parser.ts that scans ahead.

The detection needs to know:
1. Is current token `using` (for `isUsing`) or `await` (for `isAwaitUsing`)?
2. Is there a line break between current token and the next word?
3. For `using`: the next word should be a binding identifier (or `{` for statement case) on the same line. For for-head, only binding identifier (plus
`of` special case).
4. For `await using`: after `await`, the next word is `using` (same line), then after `using` a binding identifier (same line). Also `await` itself —
current token is `AwaitKeyword`, so no need to check word.

Let me write a simple `isUsingDeclaration(parser, isAwaitUsing, isFor)` helper. It will scan from `parser.index` (end of current token). Since the
current token is guaranteed to be `using` or `await`, and `parser.index` is right after it, we can:
- For isAwaitUsing: skip whitespace/comments to next word; if line break -> false; if word != "using" -> false; record new position after "using".
- Skip whitespace/comments from after "using"; if line break -> false.
- For isFor: check next word is binding identifier (not `in`/`of` etc.), with special `of` handling.
- For statement: check next char starts binding identifier or is `{`.

Let me write a helper that skips whitespace and comments and returns the next "word" and whether a line break was crossed. Since we need exact
positions, let's define:

```ts
function isLineTerminator(ch: number): boolean {
  return ch === Chars.LineFeed || ch === Chars.CarriageReturn || ch === Chars.LineSeparator || ch === Chars.ParagraphSeparator;
}

function skipSpaceAndComments(parser: Parser, start: number): { pos: number; hasLineBreak: boolean } {
  let pos = start;
  let hasLineBreak = false;
  const source = parser.source;
  const end = parser.end;
  while (pos < end) {
    const ch = source.charCodeAt(pos);
    if (ch === Chars.LineFeed || ch === Chars.CarriageReturn || ch === Chars.LineSeparator || ch === Chars.ParagraphSeparator) {
      hasLineBreak = true;
      pos++;
      continue; // continue skipping whitespace after newline
    }
    if (ch === Chars.Slash && source.charCodeAt(pos+1) === Chars.Slash) {
      // single line comment
      pos += 2;
      while (pos < end && !isLineTerminator(source.charCodeAt(pos))) pos++;
      continue;
    }
    if (ch === Chars.Slash && source.charCodeAt(pos+1) === Chars.Asterisk) {
      pos += 2;
      while (pos < end) {
        if (source.charCodeAt(pos) === Chars.Asterisk && source.charCodeAt(pos+1) === Chars.Slash) { pos += 2; break; }
        if (isLineTerminator(source.charCodeAt(pos))) hasLineBreak = true;
        pos++;
      }
      continue;
    }
    if (isECMAScriptWhitespace(ch)) {
      pos++;
      continue;
    }
    break;
  }
  return { pos, hasLineBreak };
}
```

Wait, but for `await\nusing foo`, the line break occurs before `using`; we should return false (because no line break allowed between await and
using). The helper above returns hasLineBreak true, and we check it. Good.

But there's a subtlety: for `using\nfoo`, the line break occurs between using and foo -> false (not a declaration). For comments with line breaks,
also false.

```ts
  // check identifier start (ASCII or via unicode)
  const code = source.charCodeAt(pos);
  if (!isIDStart(code)) return null; // use imported isIDStart?
  ...
}
```

We can use `isIDStart` from `../unicode`. But identifier can also start with `\` (unicode escape) and `$` and `_`. `$` and `_` are handled by
`isIDStart`? Let's check — unicode table includes `$` and `_` likely yes. And `\` (backslash) starts escaped identifier. For our detection, we mainly
need to know if the next token starts a binding identifier. If it starts with `\`, that's an escaped identifier; Babel treats it as valid binding
identifier (returns true for `ch === 92`). But an escaped `using`/`await` would not be a valid using keyword anyway. For simplicity, we can check
`isIDStart(code) || code === Chars.Backslash`.

Now, the key part: detection should also handle the case where the next word is a reserved keyword like `in`, `of`, `instanceof`, `let`, `const`, etc.
For `using` declaration detection, the binding identifier cannot be a reserved word. But in practice, when we consume and parse via
`parseAndClassifyIdentifier`, reserved words would be reported as errors. However, detection itself needs to distinguish `for (using in obj)` (using
as identifier) from `for (using in = x; ;)` (using declaration binding named `in`? No, `in` can't be a binding identifier). So we should exclude
reserved words from detection.

Let me look at how `isValidIdentifier` and `isStrictReservedWord` work, and whether we can classify a token as a binding identifier. In the parser,
once we consume `using` and the next token is e.g. `InKeyword`, we can check `isValidIdentifier(context, token)`. But for raw source detection, we can
map the next word to a token via `descKeywordTable`. Hmm.

Actually, maybe the simplest robust approach: implement detection using actual tokenization by consuming tokens, but handle the fallback carefully.
Let me think through the fallback for `await using`.

For `await` at statement start:
1. If `isAwaitUsing()` (raw source lookahead) returns true, parse as `await using` declaration.
2. Else, fall through to existing `parseAwaitExpressionOrIdentifier`.

For `using` at statement start:
1. If `isUsingDeclaration()` returns true, parse as using declaration.
2. Else, fall through to normal identifier/expression parsing.

So we only need raw source lookahead as a boolean; the actual parsing consumes tokens normally. This is exactly what Babel/acorn do. So let's
implement the raw source lookahead helper. It doesn't need to be perfect about all unicode edge cases; it just needs to detect `using`/`await using`
followed by an identifier start.

Let me look at what `parser.index` represents: after scanning a token, `parser.index` is the position just after the token's last character. So
scanning from `parser.index` gives us the whitespace after the current token.

Now let's design the helper in detail.

```ts
function isUsingDeclaration(parser: Parser, isAwaitUsing: 0 | 1, isFor: 0 | 1): boolean {
  if (!parser.options.next) return false;
  if (isAwaitUsing) {
    if (parser.getToken() !== Token.AwaitKeyword) return false;
  } else {
    if (parser.getToken() !== Token.UsingKeyword) return false;
  }

  const source = parser.source;
  const end = parser.end;
  let pos = parser.index;

  // skip whitespace/comments from after 'await' or 'using'
  const afterFirst = skipSpaceAndComments(parser, pos);
  if (afterFirst.hasLineBreak) return false;

  if (isAwaitUsing) {
    // expect 'using'
    if (source.startsWith('using', afterFirst.pos)) {
      // ensure it's a whole word
      const after = afterFirst.pos + 5;
      if (after < end && isIdentifierPart(source.charCodeAt(after))) return false;
      pos = after;
      const afterUsing = skipSpaceAndComments(parser, pos);
      if (afterUsing.hasLineBreak) return false;
      pos = afterUsing.pos;
    } else {
      return false;
    }
  }

  // Now check the binding identifier after 'using'
  const ch = source.charCodeAt(pos);
  if (ch === Chars.LeftBrace) {
    // statement-level using { ... } triggers destructuring error
    return !isFor;
  }
  if (!(isIDStart(ch) || ch === Chars.Backslash)) return false;

  // read the identifier word
  let wordStart = pos;
  if (ch === Chars.Backslash) {
    // escaped identifier - treat as binding identifier (rare)
    return true;
  }
  pos++;
  while (pos < end && isIdentifierPart(source.charCodeAt(pos))) pos++;
  const word = source.slice(wordStart, pos);

  // Exclude reserved relational keywords that would make this an expression
  if (word === 'in' || word === 'instanceof') return false;
  if (isFor && word === 'of') return false;
  // If it's a reserved word like 'let', 'const', 'var', 'if', 'for', etc., it can't be a binding identifier
  // but the parser will report an appropriate error if we enter declaration parsing. However detection matters
  // for distinguishing `for (using in x)` vs declaration. For statement `using let = x` Babel reports invalid let binding.
  // Let's keep it simple: only exclude in/instanceof/of (and perhaps other reserved words).
  // Actually `using let = x` should be a using declaration with invalid binding `let`. So we should NOT exclude it.
  return true;
}
```

Wait, for statement-level `using {x} = foo`, Babel detects it as using declaration. So `isFor=false`, ch === `{` -> return true. For `for (using {x}
of y)`, Babel does NOT detect as using declaration (isFor=true, ch === `{` -> return false). Good.

Now what about `using [x] = foo`? ch === `[`, not idStart, not `{` -> return false. So treated as expression. Good.

For `using foo = null` at statement level, ch = `f`, idStart, word = `foo` -> return true.

For `for (using x of y)`, ch = `x`, word = `x`, isFor true, word != 'of' -> return true. Good.
For `for (using of y)`, ch = `o`, word = `of`, isFor true -> return false. Good.
For `for (using in y)`, word = `in` -> return false. Good.
For `for (using x in y)`, word = `x` -> return true (then for-in error later).
For `for (using x of = ...)` — not relevant.

What about `using in = x;`? At statement level, word = `in`, we return false (because `in` is excluded). So `using in = x` would be parsed as
expression `using in = x` which is invalid (in operator + assignment). Babel reported "Unexpected token" for `function f(){ using in = x; }`? Actually
earlier Babel test: `function f(){ using in = x; }` -> ERR: Unexpected token. So Babel also doesn't treat `using in = x` as a using declaration
(because `in` is a relational keyword). Our behavior matches.

What about `using let = x;`? word = `let`, not excluded -> return true -> using declaration -> parseAndClassifyIdentifier will report invalid let
binding. Babel: `function f(){ using let = x; }` -> ERR: 'let' is disallowed as a lexically bound name. Good.

What about `using async = x;`? word = `async` -> return true -> using declaration binding `async`. Babel allows. Good.

What about `using await = x;`? word = `await` -> return true. In async context, `await` can't be a binding; in non-async it's fine. Babel: `function
f(){ using await = x; }` -> OK. Good.

Now, there's a subtlety with `isAwaitUsing` and raw scan: `parser.index` after `await` token. The helper needs to skip comments/whitespace. Let me
implement `skipSpaceAndComments` carefully.

Actually, I realize there might be a simpler way that leverages the existing lexer's comment skipping logic without mutating state. But writing a
small local scanner is fine.

   501	    expr = parseSequenceExpression(parser, context, privateScope, 0, tokenStart, expr);
   502	  }
   503	
   504	  /**
   505	   * ExpressionStatement[Yield, Await]:
   506	   *  [lookahead ∉ { {, function, async [no LineTerminator here] function, class, let [ }]Expression[+In, ?Yield, ?Await]
   507	   */
   508	
   509	  return parseExpressionStatement(parser, context, expr, tokenStart);
   510	}
   511	
   512	/**
   513	 * Parses block statement
   514	 *
   515	 * @see [Link](https://tc39.github.io/ecma262/#prod-BlockStatement)
   516	 * @see [Link](https://tc39.github.io/ecma262/#prod-Block)
   517	 *
   518	 * @param parser  Parser object
   519	 * @param context Context masks
   520	 * @param scope  Scope object
   521	 * @param labels Labels object
   522	 * @param type BlockStatement or StaticBlock
   523	 */
   524	function parseBlock<T extends ESTree.BlockStatement | ESTree.StaticBlock = ESTree.BlockStatement>(
   525	  parser: Parser,
   526	  context: Context,
   527	  scope: Scope | undefined,
   528	  privateScope: PrivateScope | undefined,
   529	  labels: ESTree.Labels,
   530	  start: Location = parser.tokenStart,
   531	  type: T['type'] = 'BlockStatement',
   532	): T {
   533	  // Block ::
   534	  //   '{' StatementList '}'
   535	
   536	  const body: ESTree.Statement[] = [];
   537	
   538	  consume(parser, context | Context.AllowRegExp, Token.LeftBrace);
   539	  while (parser.getToken() !== Token.RightBrace) {
   540	    body.push(
   541	      parseStatementListItem(parser, context, scope, privateScope, Origin.BlockStatement, { $: labels }) as any,
   542	    );
   543	  }
   544	
   545	  consume(parser, context | Context.AllowRegExp, Token.RightBrace);
   546	
   547	  return parser.finishNode(
   548	    {
   549	      type,
   550	      body,

REASONING_CONTENT
So blocks pass the same context (including InGlobal) but they create a child scope. We can't use context alone to determine "global scope". We need to
know whether the current statement is directly in the top-level statement list vs inside a block.

How can we tell? In `parseStatementList` (script top-level), `origin` is `Origin.TopLevel` and `scope` is the top-level scope. In a block,
`parseStatementListItem` is called with `Origin.BlockStatement`. So we can use `origin === Origin.TopLevel` (or `origin & Origin.TopLevel`) AND not
module to detect script global scope.

Wait, but `parseStatementList` calls `parseStatementListItem(..., Origin.TopLevel, ...)`. And `parseModuleItemList` also calls
`parseStatementListItem(..., Origin.TopLevel, ...)` but with module context. So script global scope = `origin & Origin.TopLevel && !(context &
Context.Module)`.

But what about top-level inside a `switch`? `parseSwitchCase` likely uses `Origin.BlockStatement`. And inside a block `origin` is
`Origin.BlockStatement`. So `origin & Origin.TopLevel` is only true for the very top-level statement list. That matches Babel's `SCOPE_TOP` check.

Let me verify: in `parseStatementList`, `parseStatementListItem(parser, context, scope, undefined, Origin.TopLevel, {})`. Yes. Good.

So the global-scope error check: if `origin & Origin.TopLevel && !(context & Context.Module)` → report script-global error.

For `await using`, priority: at script top-level, report async-context error first (not script-global). So in the `await using` parsing path, check
async context before global scope. Actually Babel checks `allowsUsing()` (global scope) first, then `recordAwaitIfAllowed()`. The task says the
opposite priority: await-context error first. So we'll check:
1. If `await using` and not in await context (and not module top-level) → report "only allowed inside async" (regardless of global scope).
2. Else if script global scope → report global scope error.

Actually wait — for `await using` at script top-level, we should report async error. So:
```ts
if (isAwaitUsing) {
  if (!isAwaitContextAllowed(context)) parser.report(Errors.AwaitUsingOutsideAsync);
  // don't check global scope if already reported
}
if (origin & Origin.TopLevel && !(context & Context.Module)) parser.report(Errors.UsingDeclarationGlobalScope);
```

But if `await using` is in an async function, then it's allowed; and if that async function is at top-level script (origin TopLevel? No — inside
function body, origin is not TopLevel; parseFunctionBody uses Origin.BlockStatement or similar). So fine.

What counts as await context? `Context.InAwaitContext` or module top-level (`Context.Module && Context.InGlobal`). Let's define:
```ts
function isAwaitUsingAllowed(context: Context): boolean {
  return (context & Context.InAwaitContext) > 0 || ((context & Context.Module) > 0 && (context & Context.InGlobal) > 0);
}
```
Note: inside a module, even inside a function body, `InAwaitContext` is set for async functions, but module top-level has `Module | InGlobal` without
`InAwaitContext`. For `await using` inside a module block (e.g., `{ await using x = y; }` at module top level), is that allowed? `await` expressions
are allowed at module top level even inside blocks? Actually `await` expressions are allowed in modules at top level and in async functions; inside a
block at module top-level, `await` is allowed too (it's still module top-level "await context"). Let me check how Meriyah handles `await` in a block
at module top-level. Let me look at `parseAwaitExpressionOrIdentifier` — it checks `context & Context.InAwaitContext || (context & Context.Module &&
context & Context.InGlobal)`. The `InGlobal` is passed down through blocks (as we saw). So `await` is allowed in a block at module top-level.
Similarly, `await using` inside a block at module top-level should be allowed. Our `isAwaitUsingAllowed` with `Module && InGlobal` covers that because
blocks retain InGlobal. Good.

### 2. Add helper functions

Add near parseStatementListItem or near the variable declaration section:

```ts
function isLineTerminatorChar(ch: number): boolean {
  return ch === Chars.LineFeed || ch === Chars.CarriageReturn || ch === Chars.LineSeparator || ch === Chars.ParagraphSeparator;
}

function isECMAScriptWhitespaceChar(ch: number): boolean {
  return ch === Chars.Space || (ch >= Chars.Tab && ch <= Chars.CarriageReturn) || isExoticECMAScriptWhitespace(ch);
}
```

Wait, `isExoticECMAScriptWhitespace` already handles U+0009, U+000B, U+000C, U+FEFF and Unicode Zs. Let me check: it includes `ch === NonBreakingSpace
|| ch === ZeroWidthNoBreakSpace || ch === NextLine || ...`. It doesn't include Tab/LF/CR. So:
```ts
function isWhiteSpaceChar(ch: number): boolean {
  return ch === Chars.Space || ch === Chars.Tab || ch === Chars.VerticalTab || ch === Chars.FormFeed || isExoticECMAScriptWhitespace(ch);
}
```
Line terminators are handled separately.

Now the scan-ahead function:

```ts
function skipToNextToken(parser: Parser, start: number): { pos: number; hasLineBreak: boolean } {
  const source = parser.source;
  const end = parser.end;
  let pos = start;
  let hasLineBreak = false;

  while (pos < end) {
    const ch = source.charCodeAt(pos);
    if (isLineTerminatorChar(ch)) {
      hasLineBreak = true;
      pos++;
      continue;
    }
    if (isWhiteSpaceChar(ch)) {
      pos++;
      continue;
    }
    if (ch === Chars.Slash && source.charCodeAt(pos + 1) === Chars.Slash) {
      pos += 2;
      while (pos < end && !isLineTerminatorChar(source.charCodeAt(pos))) pos++;
      continue;
    }
    if (ch === Chars.Slash && source.charCodeAt(pos + 1) === Chars.Asterisk) {
      pos += 2;
      while (pos < end) {
        if (source.charCodeAt(pos) === Chars.Asterisk && source.charCodeAt(pos + 1) === Chars.Slash) {
          pos += 2;
          break;
        }
        if (isLineTerminatorChar(source.charCodeAt(pos))) hasLineBreak = true;
        pos++;
      }
      continue;
    }
    break;
  }
  return { pos, hasLineBreak };
}
```

Then the detection:

```ts
function isUsingDeclaration(parser: Parser, isAwaitUsing: 0 | 1, isFor: 0 | 1): boolean {
  if (!parser.options.next) return false;
  if (isAwaitUsing) {
    if (parser.getToken() !== Token.AwaitKeyword) return false;
  } else if (parser.getToken() !== Token.UsingKeyword) {
    return false;
  }

  const source = parser.source;
  const end = parser.end;
  let pos = parser.index;

  // skip after `await` or `using`
  let ahead = skipToNextToken(parser, pos);
  if (ahead.hasLineBreak) return false;
  pos = ahead.pos;

  if (isAwaitUsing) {
    // expect `using`
    if (source.charCodeAt(pos) !== Chars.LowerU) return false;
    if (source.slice(pos, pos + 5) !== 'using') return false;
    const after = pos + 5;
    if (after < end && isIDPart(source.charCodeAt(after))) return false;
    ahead = skipToNextToken(parser, after);
    if (ahead.hasLineBreak) return false;
    pos = ahead.pos;
  }

  const ch = source.charCodeAt(pos);
  if (ch === Chars.LeftBrace) {
    return !isFor;
  }
  if (!(isIDStart(ch) || ch === Chars.Backslash)) return false;

  // read identifier word
  let wordEnd = pos + 1;
  if (ch !== Chars.Backslash) {
    while (wordEnd < end && isIDPart(source.charCodeAt(wordEnd))) wordEnd++;
    const word = source.slice(pos, wordEnd);
    if (word === 'in' || word === 'instanceof') return false;
    if (isFor && word === 'of') return false;
  }
  return true;
}
```

Need `isIDPart` from '../unicode' (it's `isIDContinue`? Let me check: unicode.ts exports `isIDContinue`; lexer uses `isIdentifierPart` from
charClassifier which likely wraps isIDContinue). I'll import `isIDContinue` as `isIDPart` or just use it directly. Actually there's `isIdPart`
exported from charClassifier too. Let me check charClassifier exports.

### 3. Statement dispatch

In `parseStatementListItem`, add:

Wait, `switch` with `case` inside — if it's not a declaration, we want to fall through to `default` which calls parseStatement. But we can't easily

```ts
case Token.UsingKeyword:
  return isUsingDeclaration(parser, 0, 0)
    ? parseUsingDeclaration(parser, context, scope, privateScope, origin, 0)
    : parseStatement(parser, context, scope, privateScope, origin, labels, 1);

case Token.AwaitKeyword:
  if (isUsingDeclaration(parser, 1, 0)) {
    return parseUsingDeclaration(parser, context, scope, privateScope, origin, 1);
  }
  return parseStatement(parser, context, scope, privateScope, origin, labels, 1);
```

But careful: `parseStatement` when called with `AwaitKeyword`/`UsingKeyword` will go to default case and parse as expression statement. That's correct
fallback.

But there's a subtlety: `parseStatementListItem` is called for declaration positions. For `using` in a non-declaration context (e.g., body of an if
statement), `parseStatement` is called directly, not `parseStatementListItem`. Should `using` be allowed there? The task says "using may appear in any

So `using` as a single-statement body is invalid ("Lexical declaration cannot appear in a single-statement context"). Since our dispatch for
`parseStatement` default case won't recognize `using` as a declaration, it will parse `using y = z` as an expression and likely fail with some error.

`parseStatementListItem`, which is correct for statement-list positions.

Now, `parseUsingDeclaration` function:

```ts
function parseUsingDeclaration(
  parser: Parser,
  context: Context,
  scope: Scope | undefined,
  privateScope: PrivateScope | undefined,
  origin: Origin,
  isAwaitUsing: 0 | 1,
): ESTree.VariableDeclaration {
  const start = parser.tokenStart;
  const kind = isAwaitUsing ? BindingKind.AwaitUsing : BindingKind.Using;

  // Check context constraints before consuming? The error location should be at the declaration start.
  if (isAwaitUsing && !isAwaitUsingAllowed(context)) {
    parser.report(Errors.AwaitUsingOutsideAsync);
  }
  if (origin & Origin.TopLevel && !(context & Context.Module)) {
    parser.report(Errors.UsingDeclarationGlobalScope);
  }

  nextToken(parser, context); // consume `using` or `await`
  if (isAwaitUsing) {
    // `await` was the current token; after nextToken we're at `using`. Consume it.
    nextToken(parser, context);
  }
  const declarations = parseVariableDeclarationList(parser, context, scope, privateScope, kind, origin);
  matchOrInsertSemicolon(parser, context | Context.AllowRegExp);
  return parser.finishNode<ESTree.VariableDeclaration>(
    {
      type: 'VariableDeclaration',
      kind: isAwaitUsing ? 'await using' : 'using',
      declarations,
    },
    start,
  );
}
```

Wait, for error priority: `await using` at script top-level should report async error, not global. So check async first. Good. But if `await using` is
in script top-level, after reporting async error, it throws, so global won't be checked. Good.

But there's another subtlety: for `await using` in a module, we don't want global scope error (module allows). Good.

Now, `parseVariableDeclarationList` calls `parseVariableDeclaration` for each declarator. We need to:
- Require initializer for using (unless in for-of/for-in where init is null).
- Disallow destructuring for using.
- In for-in, report ForInUsing.

Let's modify `parseVariableDeclaration`:

Current:
```ts
if (parser.getToken() === Token.Assign) {
  nextToken(...);
  init = parseExpression(...);
  if (origin & Origin.ForStatement || (token & Token.IsPatternStart) === 0) {
    // check for-in/of initializer
  }
} else if (
  (kind & BindingKind.Const || (token & Token.IsPatternStart) > 0) &&
  (parser.getToken() & Token.IsInOrOf) !== Token.IsInOrOf
) {
  parser.report(Errors.DeclarationMissingInitializer, kind & BindingKind.Const ? 'const' : 'destructuring');
}
```

We need to add:
- If kind is Using or AwaitUsing and no initializer and next isn't `in`/`of` → report UsingMissingInitializer.
- If kind is Using/AwaitUsing and id is a pattern → report UsingHasBindingPattern. Actually the id parsing happens before. If the next token is `{`
(statement case), `parseBindingPattern` will parse an ObjectPattern, then we need to detect and report. Let me add after `parseBindingPattern`:
```ts
if (kind & (BindingKind.Using | BindingKind.AwaitUsing) && (token & Token.IsPatternStart) > 0) {
  parser.report(Errors.UsingHasBindingPattern);
}
```
But `parseBindingPattern` already parsed the pattern and may have consumed it. For `using {x} = foo`, `parseBindingPattern` parses `{x}` as pattern;
then we report destructuring error. That's fine. But wait — does `parseBindingPattern` do scope registration for pattern elements? It may add
bindings, but since we immediately throw, it doesn't matter much.

Actually, we should report destructuring before parsing the initializer. The position in Babel is at the pattern start. In our code, after
`parseBindingPattern`, `parser.tokenStart` would be at the `=` (or next token). The error location might be off but that's okay as long as message
substring is present. Actually let's report at the id start. The `tokenStart` variable at function start is the start of the declarator (binding). For
`using {x}`, tokenStart is at `{`. We can use that for the error. Let me report `UsingHasBindingPattern` right after parsing id (or even before
parsing it, since we know the token is pattern start). Better: report before parsing to avoid side effects. But we need `id` for AST; since we're
throwing, doesn't matter. Let's report immediately after we determine `token & Token.IsPatternStart` and kind is using.

Actually for for-head `for (using {x} of y)`, isUsingDeclaration(false, isFor=1) returns false (because `{` not accepted in for). So that case won't
reach parseUsingDeclaration; it'll be parsed as expression and likely error differently. Fine.

For statement `using {x} = foo`, isUsingDeclaration returns true; parseUsingDeclaration -> parseVariableDeclarationList -> parseVariableDeclaration ->
parseBindingPattern with `token = LeftBrace`. We can report `UsingHasBindingPattern` before calling parseBindingPattern:
```ts
const token = parser.getToken();
if (kind & (BindingKind.Using | BindingKind.AwaitUsing) && (token & Token.IsPatternStart) > 0) {
  parser.report(Errors.UsingHasBindingPattern);
}
const id = parseBindingPattern(...);
```
This way we don't parse the pattern. Good.

- Missing initializer: for using, if no `=` and next token is not `in`/`of`, report UsingMissingInitializer. For for-of (`for (using x of y)`), no
initializer but next is `of` → allowed (init null). For for-in, next is `in` → allowed at parseVariableDeclaration level, but later parseForStatement
should report ForInUsing. Actually Babel reports for-in error after parsing. Let's allow missing initializer when next is `in`/`of`, and then report
ForInUsing in parseForStatement.

Let me modify the missing-initializer condition:
```ts
} else if (
  (kind & (BindingKind.Const | BindingKind.Using | BindingKind.AwaitUsing) || (token & Token.IsPatternStart) > 0) &&
  (parser.getToken() & Token.IsInOrOf) !== Token.IsInOrOf
) {
  if (kind & (BindingKind.Using | BindingKind.AwaitUsing)) {
    parser.report(Errors.UsingMissingInitializer);
  }
  parser.report(Errors.DeclarationMissingInitializer, kind & BindingKind.Const ? 'const' : 'destructuring');
}
```

### For loop handling

In `parseForStatement`, after `consume(... LeftParen)`:
- Current code computes `isVarDecl` based on var/let/const.
- We need to detect `using` and `await using`.

Add:
```ts
const usingKind = isUsingDeclaration(parser, 1, 1) ? 1 : isUsingDeclaration(parser, 0, 1) ? 0 : -1;
```
Wait, order matters: for `for (await using x of y)`, current token is `AwaitKeyword`. `isUsingDeclaration(parser, 1, 1)` checks await followed by
using. For `for (using x of y)`, current token is `UsingKeyword`, so `isUsingDeclaration(parser, 0, 1)` checks using. We need to check await-using
first (so `await using` is not mistaken). Let me set:
```ts
let usingDecl = -1; // -1 = not using, 0 = using, 1 = await using
if (isUsingDeclaration(parser, 1, 1)) usingDecl = 1;
else if (isUsingDeclaration(parser, 0, 1)) usingDecl = 0;
```

Then if `usingDecl >= 0`, build a VariableDeclaration:
```ts
if (usingDecl >= 0) {
  const isAwaitUsing = usingDecl === 1;
  // Check await context
  if (isAwaitUsing && !isAwaitUsingAllowed(context)) {
    parser.report(Errors.AwaitUsingOutsideAsync);
  }
  const kind = isAwaitUsing ? BindingKind.AwaitUsing : BindingKind.Using;
  const tokenStart2 = parser.tokenStart;
  nextToken(parser, context); // consume using (or await)
  if (isAwaitUsing) nextToken(parser, context); // consume using
  init = parser.finishNode<ESTree.VariableDeclaration>(
    {
      type: 'VariableDeclaration',
      kind: isAwaitUsing ? 'await using' : 'using',
      declarations: parseVariableDeclarationList(
        parser,
        context | Context.DisallowIn,
        scope,
        privateScope,
        kind,
        Origin.ForStatement,
      ),
    },
    tokenStart2,
  );
  parser.assignable = AssignmentKind.Assignable;
}
```

Wait, but the for-head can also be `for await (using x of y)` or `for await (await using x of y)`. The `forAwait` logic already consumes the first
`await` after `for`. Then inside parens, current token is `using` or `await`. The above detection handles it.

But there's a conflict with the existing `isVarDecl` path. Let me restructure `parseForStatement` to handle using before `isVarDecl`. Let me look at
the current flow again:

```ts
let isVarDecl = token is var/let/const;
const { tokenStart } = parser;
const token = parser.getToken();

if (isVarDecl) {
  ...
} else if (token === Token.Semicolon) {
  ...
} else if ((token & Token.IsPatternStart) === Token.IsPatternStart) {
  ...
} else {
  init = parseLeftHandSideExpression(...);
}
```

I'll add a using branch before `if (isVarDecl)`:

```ts
let usingDecl = ...;
if (usingDecl >= 0) {
  ... build init ...
  isVarDecl = true; // so later for(;;) handling treats it as var decl
} else if (isVarDecl) {
  ...
}
```

Wait, `isVarDecl` is used later:
- In the `in`/`of` branch, it doesn't matter much.
- After the in/of branch: `if (!isVarDecl) { ... parseAssignmentExpression ... }`.
So for using in a classic for (`for (using x = 0; ; )`), we need `isVarDecl` true so it skips the assignment-expression reparse. Let's set `isVarDecl
= true` when using detected.

But there's a complication: `parseVariableDeclarationList` for using calls `parseVariableDeclaration` which, when `origin & Origin.ForStatement` and
the current token is `of` or `in`, will allow missing initializer. Good.

Also, the `parseVariableDeclarationList` has:
```ts
if (bindingCount > 1 && origin & Origin.ForStatement && parser.getToken() & Token.IsInOrOf) {
  parser.report(Errors.ForInOfLoopMultiBindings, ...);
}
```
This works for using too.

Then in the `in`/`of` branch:
```ts
if ((parser.getToken() & Token.IsInOrOf) === Token.IsInOrOf) {
  if (parser.getToken() === Token.OfKeyword) {
    ...
  }
  // For in:
  if (parser.assignable & AssignmentKind.CannotAssign) ...

  ...
  return parser.finishNode ForInStatement...
}
```
We need to add: if the init is a using/await using declaration and current token is `in`, report `ForInUsing`.

How to detect that init is a using declaration? We can set a flag `usingForIn = usingDecl >= 0 && parser.getToken() === Token.InKeyword`. Or check
`init.kind`. Since `init` is typed loosely, we can check `init?.kind`. But the `in`/`of` branch is shared with expression/let/const. Let me add a
variable `isUsingInFor = usingDecl >= 0` and check inside the `in` branch.

Actually, the for-in error should be reported for both `using` and `await using` declarations when `in` is used. Let me add in the for-in handling
(the non-of branch):
```ts
if (usingDecl >= 0) parser.report(Errors.ForInUsing);
```
Place it before or after `forAwait` check? Babel reports ForInUsing regardless. Let's report before `reinterpretToPattern`.

But note: `for (using x in y)` — isUsingDeclaration(parser, 0, 1) returns true (word `x`, isFor true, not `of`), so usingDecl=0, init built. Then at
`in`, we report ForInUsing. Good.

What about `for (using in y)`? isUsingDeclaration returns false (word `in` excluded), so usingDecl=-1, and it goes to expression parsing → `using`
identifier as for-in left side. No error. Good.

### `for await` invalid when using without for-of
If `for await (using x; ; )`? That's invalid anyway (for await requires of). The existing code reports InvalidForAwait at the end. Fine.

### Check: `for (await using x in y)` in module
usingDecl=1, init built, `in` branch → report ForInUsing. Good. No await-context error because module allowed.

### Check: `for (await using x = 0; ;)` in non-async
usingDecl=1, check isAwaitUsingAllowed → false → report AwaitUsingOutsideAsync. Good.

### Now statement-level `await using` detection

In `parseStatementListItem`, add `case Token.AwaitKeyword`:
```ts
case Token.AwaitKeyword:
  if (isUsingDeclaration(parser, 1, 0)) {
    return parseUsingDeclaration(parser, context, scope, privateScope, origin, 1);
  }
  return parseStatement(parser, context, scope, privateScope, origin, labels, 1);
```

But wait — `parseStatement` with `AwaitKeyword` will go to its default case and call `parsePrimaryExpression` → `parseAwaitExpressionOrIdentifier`.
That's the correct fallback.

However, there's a subtlety: `parseStatement` has a switch with `Token.AwaitKeyword`? Let me check the full switch in parseStatement — I saw default
goes to parsePrimaryExpression. No explicit await case. So fallback works.

For `case Token.UsingKeyword` in parseStatementListItem:
```ts
case Token.UsingKeyword:
  if (isUsingDeclaration(parser, 0, 0)) {
    return parseUsingDeclaration(parser, context, scope, privateScope, origin, 0);
  }
  return parseStatement(parser, context, scope, privateScope, origin, labels, 1);
```

Now, there's also `parseStatement` itself — should `using` declaration be allowed inside `if` body etc.? No (single-statement context invalid). But
what about `parseStatement` being called from `parseStatementListItem` default? Actually `parseStatementListItem`'s default calls `parseStatement`. So
if `UsingKeyword` is not matched in parseStatementListItem (we now match it), fine.

Wait — but `parseStatement` is called directly for statement bodies (e.g., if body, while body). Should we add using handling there? No —
single-statement contexts should reject using declarations. So leave parseStatement as is; `using` will be parsed as expression and fail appropriately
(e.g., `using y = z` as expression might actually parse as `using` identifier, then `y = z`... actually `using y = z` as expression statement would be
invalid — `using` followed by `y` without operator; parseMemberOrUpdateExpression would fail). Good enough.

### Now, the `{` destructuring case at statement level

`using {x} = foo` — isUsingDeclaration(parser, 0, 0) returns true (ch === `{`). parseUsingDeclaration → parseVariableDeclarationList →
parseVariableDeclaration. In parseVariableDeclaration, we report UsingHasBindingPattern before parsing pattern. But wait, `parseBindingPattern` is
called before the check I planned. Let me restructure parseVariableDeclaration to check pattern for using before parsing. Let me look at the function
again and edit.

Actually there's another subtlety: for `using {x} = foo`, after `nextToken` in parseUsingDeclaration (consuming `using`), current token is
`LeftBrace`. parseVariableDeclarationList → parseVariableDeclaration with `token = LeftBrace`. We report UsingHasBindingPattern. Good.

Now add helper functions. I'll insert them after the import block, before `parseSource`. Let me add:

```ts
function isUsingLineTerminator(ch: number): boolean {
  return ch === Chars.LineFeed || ch === Chars.CarriageReturn || ch === Chars.LineSeparator || ch === Chars.ParagraphSeparator;
}

function isUsingWhiteSpace(ch: number): boolean {
  return ch === Chars.Space || ch === Chars.Tab || ch === Chars.VerticalTab || ch === Chars.FormFeed || isExoticECMAScriptWhitespace(ch);
}

function skipToNextToken(parser: Parser, start: number): { pos: number; hasLineBreak: boolean } { ... }

function isUsingDeclaration(parser: Parser, isAwaitUsing: 0 | 1, isFor: 0 | 1): boolean { ... }

function isAwaitUsingAllowed(context: Context): boolean { ... }
```

A using declaration requires an identifier (or object pattern,\n * outside of for-heads) to follow on the same line.\n */\nfunction isUsingLineTerminator(ch: number): boolean {\n  return ch === Chars.LineFeed || ch === Chars.CarriageReturn || ch === Chars.LineSeparator || ch === Chars.ParagraphSeparator;\n}\n\nfunction isUsingWhiteSpace(ch: number): boolean {\n  return ch === Chars.Space || ch === Chars.Tab || ch === Chars.VerticalTab || ch === Chars.FormFeed || isExoticECMAScriptWhitespace(ch);\n}\n\nfunction skipToNextToken(parser: Parser, start: number): { pos: number; hasLineBreak: boolean } {\n  const { source, end } = parser;\n  let pos = start;\n  let hasLineBreak = false;\n\n  while (pos < end) {\n    const ch = source.charCodeAt(pos);\n    if (isUsingLineTerminator(ch)) {\n      hasLineBreak = true;\n      pos++;\n    } else if (isUsingWhiteSpace(ch)) {\n      pos++;\n    } else if (ch === Chars.Slash && source.charCodeAt(pos + 1) === Chars.Slash) {\n      pos += 2;\n      while (pos < end && !isUsingLineTerminator(source.charCodeAt(pos))) pos++;\n    } else if (ch === Chars.Slash && source.charCodeAt(pos + 1) === Chars.Asterisk) {\n      pos += 2;\n      while (pos < end) {\n        if (source.charCodeAt(pos) === Chars.Asterisk && source.charCodeAt(pos + 1) === Chars.Slash) {\n          pos += 2;\n          break;\n        }\n        if (isUsingLineTerminator(source.charCodeAt(pos))) hasLineBreak = true;\n        pos++;\n      }\n    } else {\n      break;\n    }\n  }\n\n  return { pos, hasLineBreak };\n}\n\nfunction isUsingDeclaration(parser: Parser, isAwaitUsing: 0 | 1, isFor: 0 | 1): boolean {\n  if (!parser.options.next) return false;\n  if (isAwaitUsing) {\n    if (parser.getToken() !== Token.AwaitKeyword) return false;\n  } else if (parser.getToken() !== Token.UsingKeyword) {\n    return false;\n  }\n\n  const { source, end } = parser;\n  let pos = parser.index;\n\n  let ahead = skipToNextToken(parser, pos);\n  if (ahead.hasLineBreak) return false;\n  pos = ahead.pos;\n\n  if (isAwaitUsing) {\n    if (source.slice(pos, pos + 5) !== 'using') return false;\n    const afterUsing = pos + 5;\n    if (afterUsing < end && isIdentifierPart(source.charCodeAt(afterUsing))) return false;\n    ahead = skipToNextToken(parser, afterUsing);\n    if (ahead.hasLineBreak) return false;\n    pos = ahead.pos;\n as an expression.\n    return !isFor;\n  }\n\n  if (!(isIdentifierStart(ch) || ch === Chars.Backslash)) return false;\n\n  if (ch !== Chars.Backslash) {\n    let wordEnd = pos + 1;\n    while (wordEnd < end && isIdentifierPart(source.charCodeAt(wordEnd))) wordEnd++;\n    const word = source.slice(pos, wordEnd);\n    if (word === 'in' || word === 'instanceof') return false;\n    if (isFor && word === 'of') return false;\n  }\n\n  return true;\n}\n\nfunction isAwaitUsingAllowed(context: Context): boolean {\n  return (context & Context.InAwaitContext) > 0 || ((context & Context.Module) > 0 && (context & Context.InGlobal) > 0);\n}\n\n"""
assert marker in s
s = s.replace(marker, helper + marker, 1)
open(path, 'w').write(s)
print("done")

/**
 * Parses a `using` or `await using` declaration statement
 *
 * @param parser  Parser object
 * @param context Context masks
 * @param scope Scope object
 * @param origin Binding origin
 * @param isAwaitUsing Whether this is an `await using` declaration
 */
function parseUsingDeclaration(
  parser: Parser,
  context: Context,
  scope: Scope | undefined,
  privateScope: PrivateScope | undefined,
  origin: Origin,
  isAwaitUsing: 0 | 1,
): ESTree.VariableDeclaration {
  const start = parser.tokenStart;
  const kind = isAwaitUsing ? BindingKind.AwaitUsing : BindingKind.Using;

  // `await using` outside of async functions and module top-level is an error.
  // This check takes priority over the script global scope restriction.
  if (isAwaitUsing && !isAwaitUsingAllowed(context)) {
    parser.report(Errors.AwaitUsingOutsideAsync);
  }

  if ((origin & Origin.TopLevel) > 0 && (context & Context.Module) === 0) {
    parser.report(Errors.UsingDeclarationGlobalScope);
  }

  nextToken(parser, context);
  if (isAwaitUsing) nextToken(parser, context);

  const declarations = parseVariableDeclarationList(parser, context, scope, privateScope, kind, origin);

  matchOrInsertSemicolon(parser, context | Context.AllowRegExp);
  return parser.finishNode<ESTree.VariableDeclaration>(
    {
      type: 'VariableDeclaration',
      kind: isAwaitUsing ? 'await using' : 'using',
      declarations,
    },
    start,
  );
}

/**
 * Parses variable declaration list
"""
assert old in s

open(path, 'w').write(s)
print("done")

   210	 * @param parser Parser state
   211	 * @param context Context masks
   212	 * @param t The type of token to consume
   213	 */
   214	export function consume(parser: Parser, context: Context, t: Token): void {
   215	  if (parser.getToken() !== t) parser.report(Errors.ExpectedToken, KeywordDescTable[t & Token.Type]);
   216	  nextToken(parser, context);
   217	}
   218	
   219	/**
   220	 * Transforms a `LeftHandSideExpression` into a `AssignmentPattern` if possible,
   221	 * otherwise it returns the original tree.
   222	 *
   223	 * @param parser Parser state
   224	 * @param {*} node
   225	 */
   226	export function reinterpretToPattern(parser: Parser, node: any): void {
   227	  switch (node.type) {
   228	    case 'ArrayExpression': {
   229	      node.type = 'ArrayPattern';
   230	      const { elements } = node;
   231	      for (let i = 0, n = elements.length; i < n; ++i) {
   232	        const element = elements[i];
   233	        if (element) reinterpretToPattern(parser, element);
   234	      }
   235	      return;
   236	    }
   237	    case 'ObjectExpression': {
   238	      node.type = 'ObjectPattern';
   239	      const { properties } = node;
   240	      for (let i = 0, n = properties.length; i < n; ++i) {
   241	        reinterpretToPattern(parser, properties[i]);
   242	      }
   243	      return;
   244	    }
   245	    case 'AssignmentExpression':
   246	      node.type = 'AssignmentPattern';
   247	      if (node.operator !== '=') parser.report(Errors.InvalidDestructuringTarget);
   248	      delete node.operator;
   249	      reinterpretToPattern(parser, node.left);
   250	      return;
   251	    case 'Property':
   252	      reinterpretToPattern(parser, node.value);
   253	      return;
   254	    case 'SpreadElement':
   255	      node.type = 'RestElement';
   256	      reinterpretToPattern(parser, node.argument);
   257	    // No default
   258	  }
   259	}

STEP 93 SOURCE agent
REASONING_CONTENT
`reinterpretToPattern` doesn't handle VariableDeclaration, but it's called on `init` for both expression and variable declaration paths. For let/const
in for-of, init is VariableDeclaration and reinterpretToPattern just returns without doing anything (no case). So it's a no-op for
VariableDeclaration. That's fine — the id is already an Identifier/pattern. Good.

Now, there's a potential issue: in the `of` branch, `reinterpretToPattern(parser, init)` is called even for using. Since init is VariableDeclaration,
no-op. Good.

Now let's also consider: for `for (using x of y)` in script global top-level, should the global scope error apply? The task says "using may appear in
any scope including script top-level" and "For-of ... accept both using ... in their heads". Does the global-scope restriction apply to for-heads?
Babel: `for (using x of y)` at script top-level — let me check. Earlier test showed `for (using x of y) {}` with sourceType script, ecmaVersion latest
→ acorn OK, babel OK. So for-head using at script top-level is allowed even though script global `using` statement is not. Interesting! Because the
for-head creates a child scope. Babel's `allowsUsing` check is only done in parseStatementContent, not in parseForStatement (the for path doesn't
check allowsUsing). Indeed in Babel's parseForStatement, there's no `allowsUsing()` check. So `for (using x of y)` at script top-level is allowed.

Our parseForStatement path doesn't check global scope, so it's allowed. Good, matches.

Now, let me compile/typecheck to find errors. Let's run tsc.

import { parseSource } from '/app/src/parser.ts';

function run(code, options = {}) {
  try {
    const ast = parseSource(code, { next: true, ...options });
    return 'OK ' + JSON.stringify(ast.body.map(n => n.type + ':' + (n.kind || '')).join('|'));
  } catch (e) {
    return 'ERR: ' + e.description || e.message;
  }
}

const cases = [
  ['using foo = null', {}],
  ['using foo = null', {sourceType:'module'}],
  ['{ using foo = null; }', {}],
  ['function f(){ using x = 1; }', {}],
  ['await using foo = null', {}],

import { describe, it } from 'vitest';
import { parseSource } from '../src/parser';

function run(code: string, options: any = {}) {
  try {
    const ast = parseSource(code, { next: true, ...options });
    return 'OK ' + JSON.stringify(ast.body.map((n: any) => n.type + ':' + (n.kind || '')).join('|'));
  } catch (e: any) {
    return 'ERR: ' + (e.description || e.message);
  }
}

describe('quick using', () => {
  it('cases', () => {
    const cases: [string, any][] = [
      ['using foo = null', {}],
      ['using foo = null', {sourceType:'module'}],
      ['{ using foo = null; }', {}],
      ['function f(){ using x = 1; }', {}],
      ['await using foo = null', {}],
      ['await using foo = null', {sourceType:'module'}],
      ['async function f(){ await using x = 1; }', {}],
      ['for (using x of y) {}', {}],
      ['for (using x in y) {}', {}],
      ['for (await using x of y) {}', {}],
      ['for (await using x of y) {}', {sourceType:'module'}],
      ['async function f(){ for await (using x of y) {} }', {}],
      ['async function f(){ for await (await using x of y) {} }', {}],
      ['function f(){ using x; }', {}],
      ['function f(){ using {x} = y; }', {}],
      ['using {x} = y', {}],
      ['using [x] = y', {}],
      ['using\nfoo = null', {}],
      ['function f(){ using\nfoo = null; }', {}],
      ['function f(){ for (using of y) {} }', {}],
      ['function f(){ for (using in y) {} }', {}],
      ['function f(){ for (using x = 0; ; ) {} }', {}],
      ['{ await using x = y; }', {sourceType:'module'}],
    ];
    for (const [code, opts] of cases) {
      console.log(JSON.stringify(code), opts.sourceType||'', '=>', run(code, opts));
    }
  });
});
EOF

    const description = errorMessages[type].replace(/%(\d+)/g, (_: string, i: number) => params[i]);

      end: { line: end.line, column: end.column },
    };
    this.description = description;

  [Errors.UsingHasBindingPattern]: 'Using declaration cannot have destructuring patterns',
};
"""

open(path, 'w').write(s)
print("done")

STEP 109 SOURCE agent
REASONING_CONTENT
For arrow functions, `parseFunctionBody` is called with `Origin.Arrow` and context without InGlobal but WITHOUT InMethodOrFunction! So arrow function
bodies don't set InMethodOrFunction. But wait — `Origin.Arrow` is not `Origin.TopLevel`, so my proposed check `(origin & Origin.TopLevel)` would not
flag arrow bodies. But the caller at line 7034 removes InGlobal, so even the `context & InGlobal` check wouldn't flag. Actually for arrow bodies,
origin is `Origin.Arrow`, so `origin & Origin.TopLevel` is 0. Good.

But for regular function bodies, origin is `Origin.Declaration` (line 4906) or `Origin.None` (methods) — NOT TopLevel! Wait, line 4906 passes
`Origin.Declaration` to parseFunctionBody. But inside parseFunctionBody, at line 3629, it calls parseStatementListItem with `Origin.TopLevel`, not the
passed origin. So inside parseFunctionBody, the `origin` parameter is only used for the RightBrace consume logic; the statement list always uses
`Origin.TopLevel`. That's why `origin & Origin.TopLevel` was true inside function bodies.

So combining `origin & Origin.TopLevel` (from parseFunctionBody's hardcoded statement list origin) with `context & InMethodOrFunction`:
- Regular function: origin TopLevel, InMethodOrFunction 1 → no error. ✓
- Arrow function: origin TopLevel (hardcoded), InMethodOrFunction 0 → would flag error! ✗

Hmm, arrow bodies don't set InMethodOrFunction. Let me check if there's some other context set for arrow bodies. The arrow parse passes `(context &
~(InSwitch|DisallowIn|InGlobal)) | InReturnContext`. No InMethodOrFunction. So inside an arrow body, `using` would be incorrectly flagged as global.

Wait, but does the arrow body's context have InGlobal removed? Yes, line 7030 removes InGlobal. So `(context & InGlobal) > 0` is false inside arrow
bodies. Good! So combining `context & InGlobal && !(context & InMethodOrFunction)`:
- Script top-level: InGlobal 1, InMethodOrFunction 0 → error. ✓
- Block at script top: InGlobal 1, InMethodOrFunction 0 → error ✗ (should be allowed)
- Function body: InGlobal removed? Wait, in parseFunctionBody call for regular functions, context at line 4903 uses `((context | modifierFlags) ^
modifierFlags) | ...` where modifierFlags includes `InGlobal`, so InGlobal is REMOVED from function body context. So inside regular function bodies,
`context & InGlobal` is 0. Good. So the function body case wouldn't even reach the global check via InGlobal.
- Arrow body: InGlobal removed too. Good.
- Block at script top-level: InGlobal is retained (parseBlock doesn't remove it) → would still be 1 → incorrectly flagged.

So the only problematic case with `context & InGlobal && !Module && !InMethodOrFunction` is blocks at script top-level. We need `origin &
Origin.TopLevel` to exclude blocks. But then arrow bodies also use Origin.TopLevel (hardcoded) with InGlobal removed, so arrow bodies are fine because
InGlobal is removed.

Let's re-examine each:
- Script top-level: origin TopLevel, InGlobal 1, InMethodOrFunction 0, Module 0 → flag. ✓
- Block at script top: origin BlockStatement → not flag. ✓
- Function body: origin TopLevel, InGlobal 0 (removed) → not flag. ✓
- Arrow body: origin TopLevel, InGlobal 0 → not flag. ✓
- Module top-level: origin TopLevel, InGlobal 1, Module 1 → not flag. ✓
- Module block: origin BlockStatement → not flag. ✓
- Static block? Let me check what context static blocks use. Static block likely removes InGlobal. Not important.

So the correct condition is:
```ts
(origin & Origin.TopLevel) > 0 && (context & Context.InGlobal) > 0 && (context & Context.Module) === 0 && (context & Context.InMethodOrFunction) === 0
```
Actually the `InMethodOrFunction` check is redundant because function bodies have InGlobal removed. But keep it for safety. Wait, could a function
body ever retain InGlobal? In parseFunctionBody call for regular functions, modifierFlags includes InGlobal so it's removed. For methods, same. For
arrows, same. So InGlobal is always removed inside functions. So just `origin & Origin.TopLevel && context & InGlobal && !Module` suffices. But
there's a subtle case: class static blocks? They might retain InGlobal? Let me not worry; `origin` there is BlockStatement.

Let me update to use both origin and context.
